CHAPTER 5 SIGNAGE
5.01   Purpose and Intent
5.02   Definitions
5.03   Permitted Signs
5.04   Conformity of Signs
5.05   Construction Standards
5.06   Maintenance
5.07   Signs Not to Constitute a Traffic Hazard
5.08   Repair of Building Façade
5.09   Signs in Conjunction with Conditional Uses.
5.10   Lights and Lighted Signs
5.11   Prohibited Signs
5.12   Maintenance of a Clear View at an Intersection
5.13   Signs on Utility Poles, Street Trees and Street Name Poles
5.14   Monument Signs
5.15   Freeway Oriented Project Signs
5.16   Wall Signs
5.17   Wayfinding Signs
5.18   Awnings
5.19   Pylon Signs
5.20   Freeway Oriented Off-Premise Signs
5.21   Sign Approval
5.01   Purpose and Intent
MIDA has developed the regulations set forth in this Chapter for the purpose
of:
   A.   Limiting and/or reducing the visual clutter along streets;
   B.   Encouraging good design
   C.   Supporting safe driving with appropriate signage;
   D.   Regulating all types of signs that are visible from the adjacent
vehicular public right-of-way as well as I-15;
   E.   Safeguarding and protecting property values;
   F.   Promoting the public health, safety and the general welfare of the
users of Falcon Hill.
5.02   Definitions
   As used in this Chapter:
      Awnings - a roof-like structure made of cloth, metal or other material
affixed to a building for shelter or ornamental purposes in such a manner that
the structure may or may not be raised or retracted to a position against the
building and does not have ground support.
      Freeway Oriented Off-Premise Sign - a sign erected on site that is
intended to be visible from the freeway and that directs attention to a
business, commodity, service, or entertainment conducted, sold or offered at a
location on or off the premises on which the sign is located.
      Freeway Oriented Project Sign - a sign erected on site that is intended
to be visible from the freeway and provides the project name and entities in
the project.
      Monument Sign - a sign supported by a solid base with zero ground
clearance, not attached to a wall or building. This sign will be a ground sign
which is mounted in or on a monument and does not have any exposed pole or
pylon, and is attached to a base for at least sixty percent (60%) of the entire
width of the sign.
      Pylon Signs - a free standing sign that denotes a specific area. This
sign will be a ground sign which is mounted in or on a monument and does not
have any exposed pole, and is attached to a base for at least sixty percent
(60%) of the entire width of the sign.
      Wall Sign - any sign or projecting sign attached to or erected against
the wall of a building or structure or suspended from a canopy, with the
exposed face of the sign in a plane parallel to or perpendicular to the plane
of the wall.
      Wayfinding Sign - a sign erected to provide direction within the project.
5.03   Permitted Signs
Signs shall be compatible with the approved architectural theme of Falcon Hill.
Signs shall be subject to all general regulations set forth in the Development
Standards and to the following additional requirements:
   A.   Only the following signs are allowed in Falcon Hill:
      1.   Monument;
      2.   Freeway Oriented Project Signs;
      3.   Wall Signs;
      4.   Wayfinding Signs;
      5.   Awnings;
      6.   Pylons Signs;
   B.   Seasonal or coordinated event pennants and banners may be approved by
the MIDA Board.
5.04   Conformity of Signs
Except as provided in the Development Standards, no sign shall be erected,
raised, moved, placed, reconstructed, extended, enlarged or altered, except in
conformity with the regulations herein.
5.05   Construction Standards
All signs, hereinafter erected in Falcon Hill, shall comply with the current
standards of the National Electrical Code, the International Building Code, and
all provisions of the Development Standards.
5.06   Maintenance
Every sign shall be kept in good repair and properly maintained by the owner.
This includes the replacement of defective parts, repainting, cleaning, keeping
the area around the sign free of weeds and trash and other acts required for
proper maintenance. MIDA’s representative shall inspect and enforce this
Section as specified in the Development Standards.
5.07   Signs Not to Constitute a Traffic Hazard
No sign or other advertising structure shall be erected where it may interfere
with, obstruct the view of, or be confused with any authorized traffic sign,
signal or device, or which makes use of the words "STOP," "YIELD," "DANGER" or
any other words, phrases, symbol or character in such manner as to interfere
with, mislead or confuse traffic.
5.08   Repair of Building Façade
A damaged building façade, resulting from the removal, repair, replacement or
installation of any signs, shall be repaired by the property owner within 30
calendar days from the date of said damage.
5.09   Signs in conjunction with Conditional Uses
Whenever an application is made for a Site Plan review, the Applicant shall
include details of any sign(s) to be erected on the premises described in the
Site Plan review application. In reviewing the application, the MIDA Board
shall consider the character of the surrounding parcels and the sign's
compatibility with the architecture, materials, and colors of the associated
building. The MIDA Board shall either approve, apply conditions to, or deny the
application in accordance with their findings.
5.10   Lights and Lighted Signs
   A.   Lighting elements shall be hidden or utilize full-cut-off shields to
eliminate shining or reflecting up into the night sky and to minimize glare and
light trespass beyond area where lighting is needed or onto adjacent
properties.
   B.   Up-lighting shall be used sparingly for accenting architecture,
landscaping, and signing. Where up-lighting is used, a narrow angle focused
fixture with low wattage lamp shall be used.
   C.   Lighting shall be arranged or directed so as to reflect the light away
from adjacent properties and to prevent glare for street traffic. The intensity
of light at adjoining property shall not exceed 0.1 footcandles.
5.11   Prohibited Signs
The following signs are expressly prohibited:
   A.   Any mobile sign located on the property or within a building or
vehicle, such as an "A" frame, trailer sign or pedestal-type sign is
prohibited;
   B.   Cabinet signs;
   C.   Pole signs;
   D.   Animated, roof, and graffiti;
   E.   Spotlights, streamers, pennants, banners and other decorative device
for commercial advertising purposes;
   F.   Balloons, including cold air, helium, and other balloons.
   G.   Freeway Oriented Off-Premise Signs, unless an exception is made by the
MIDA Board as provided in Section
5.20.
5.12   Maintenance of a Clear View at an Intersection
   A.   A clear view area at the intersection of two (2) streets shall be
maintained within a triangular area formed by a diagonal line connecting lines
at the top back of the curbs forty (40) feet from the projected intersection of
such curb lines. To maintain this clear view no visual obstructions between two
(2) and seven (7) feet in height from the street elevation, as measured from
the elevation of the adjoining sidewalk, shall be allowed.
   B.   A clear-view area shall also be maintained at the intersection of a
street and a private drive within a triangular area formed by a diagonal line
connecting the line of the curb of the street and the line of the edge of the
private drive at points twenty (20) feet from the projected intersection of
such lines. In order to maintain this clear view, the standards outlined in
Subsection A. above shall apply.
5.13   Signs on Utility Poles, Street Trees and Street Name Poles
To ensure safe access for maintenance or emergency services by the various
companies using utility poles and to promote consistency along the streetscape,
signs shall not be located on utility poles, street trees, traffic regulatory
sign poles, and street name poles.
5.14   Monument Signs
Monument "on-premise" signs shall be allowed in Falcon Hill as a permitted use,
provided that:
   A.   A minimum setback from the public right-of-way of five (5) feet is
required;
   B.   Monument signs shall not exceed twenty-five (25) feet in height and
have a maximum of two (2) display sides;
   C.   Such signs shall be incorporated into a landscape design scheme or
planter box equal to at least twice the area of the sign, unless little
flexibility exists on the site and a waiver is granted by the Executive
Director or designee. The landscaping shall have a slope no greater than 1:
4 and the overall height of berm or planter box shall not exceed three (3)
feet;
   D.   Such signs shall generally maintain a one hundred (100) foot separation
from all other signs and eighty (80) feet from the rear property lines that are
not adjacent to a public right-of-way. In situations with minimal frontages
where very little flexibility in sign placement exists, less stringent
standards may apply as determined by the Executive Director or designee;
   E.   Such signs shall be limited to two hundred (200) square feet of display
area per side;
   F.   Such signs shall be limited to one (1) monument sign per building or
two hundred (200) feet of frontage. Properties that have more than one (1)
frontage may have at least one (1) sign per frontage if there is a cumulative
total of two hundred (200) feet of frontage or at least one hundred (100) feet
of frontage per street frontage, whichever is greater;
   G.   Such signs shall require a non-advertising base with a minimum height
of one (1) foot. Base material to match or otherwise be compatible with
material used on the primary building.
5.15   Freeway Oriented Project Signs
Freeway oriented project "on-premise" signs are a conditional use and shall
conform to the following:
   A.   Any freeway-oriented project sign within six hundred sixty (660) feet
of freeway right-of-way may extend twenty-five (25) feet above the grade of the
adjacent freeway or sixty-five (65) feet above the ground, whichever is higher.
Freeway oriented project signs shall be set back ten (10) feet from the
property line.
   B.   Only one (1) sign per freeway interchange will be allowed.
   C.   Conform to the sign standards in the design guidelines in
Appendix A, Chapter 6.
5.16   Wall Signs
Wall "on-premise" signs shall be allowed in Falcon Hill as a permitted use,
provided that:
   A.   Wall signs against buildings or other structures shall maintain a two
(2) foot clearance of roof lines, wall edges or parapet wall of a building.
When a building has more than one level, the wall on which the sign is
installed will govern.
   B.   Such signs shall not exceed ten percent (10%) of building face.
   C.   Multiple signs permitted as long as the aggregate area does not exceed
ten percent (10%) of building face.
5.17   Wayfinding Signs
Wayfinding signs shall conform to the design guidelines established in
Appendix A, Chapter 6.
5.18   Awnings
   A.   Number: There shall not be more than one awning or canopy sign for each
principal building except that where the building abuts two (2) or more
streets, additional such signs, one (1) oriented to each abutting street, shall
be permitted.
   B.   Area:
      1.   Awning or Canopy: The gross surface area of an awning or canopy sign
shall not exceed fifty percent (50%) of the gross surface area of the smallest
face of the awning or canopy to which such sign is to be affixed.
      2.   Blade Type Awning: The width for a blade type awning shall be eighty
percent (80%) of the width of the face of the blade type awning structure.
      3.   Building Face: The area of the sign shall not exceed ten percent
(10%) of the building face.
   C.   Height: An awning or canopy sign shall not project higher than the top
of the awning or canopy to which such sign is to be affixed. Projections above
or below the canopy may be permitted for blade type awnings.
5.19   Pylon Signs
Pylon "on-premise" signs shall be allowed in Falcon Hill as a permitted use,
provided that:
   A.   A minimum setback from the public right-of-way of five (5) feet is
required;
   B.   Pylon signs shall not exceed twenty-five (25) feet in height;
   C.   Such signs shall be incorporated into a landscape design scheme or
planter box equal to at least twice the area of the sign, unless little
flexibility exists on the site and a waiver is granted by the Executive
Director or designee. The landscaping shall have a slope no greater than 1:
4 and the overall height of berm or planter box shall not exceed three (3)
feet;
   D.   Such signs shall be limited to two hundred (200) square feet in area;
   E.   Such signs shall be limited to one (1) pylon sign per area entrance/
exit;
   F.   Such signs shall require a non-advertising base with a minimum height
of five (5) feet. Base material shall match or otherwise be compatible with
material used on the primary building.
5.20   Freeway Oriented Off-Premise Signs
   A.   Freeway Oriented Off-Premise Signs are prohibited, however the MIDA
Board, at its sole discretion, may make an exception and approve on a case by
case basis, a Freeway Oriented Off-Premise Sign of a temporary nature after
finding that:
      1.   It is a temporary use that does not block the view to the project
buildings;
      2.   The off-premise sign does not detract from the purpose and vision of
the project and will provide a substantial benefit to the project; and,
      3.   After reviewing all contracts related to the sign, the contractual
provisions are in harmony with the project and the temporary nature of the
sign;
5.21   Sign Approval
All allowed signs under Section
5.03(A) shall be submitted in a signage package to be reviewed by staff and the
DRC. The DRC has the authority to approve, approve with conditions, or deny the
submittal if it does not meeting the requirements of
Appendix A, Chapter 5. The signage package approval process can run
simultaneously with or separately from the site plan, but a Site Plan approval
will not be stopped if the signage plans have not been approved.
CHAPTER 6 DESIGN GOALS, GUIDELINES AND STANDARDS
6.01   Purpose
6.02   Land Use Intent
6.03   Street Hierarchy
6.04   Road Designations
6.05   Mass Transit and Alternative Transportation
6.06   Site Development
6.07   Utilities
6.08   Sustainability
6.09   Architectural Design
6.10   Landscape Design
6.11   Lighting and Night Sky Protection
6.12   Signage
6.01   Purpose
   A.   The purpose of this Chapter is to expand upon the Standards identified
in the other Chapters and provide additional mandatory standards and non-
mandatory guidelines as a means of creating harmony and unity throughout the
entire Falcon Hill project. This Chapter will assist in creating consistency
between various individual site developments within Falcon Hill. Goals and
objectives related to each element of this chapter are identified within the
standards, guidelines, and implementation options. While guidelines are not
mandatory, unlike the Standards, the intent of the guidelines shall be adhered
to so that the overall quality of the entire Falcon Hill project will be
maintained.
   B.   In this Chapter, guidelines are identified with words such as “should”
or “may.” Standards are identified by mandatory language such as “shall” or
“must.”
6.02   Land Use Intent
   A.   Objective:
      1.   The purpose and intent of Falcon Hill is to provide for a diversity
of businesses connected to aerospace and defense activities. High quality
office buildings and research facilities are the first priority of this
development and are likewise given the most visible and appropriate locations
within Falcon Hill.
      2.   Providing necessary commercial, retail, or service oriented uses
incidental to and in support of the Falcon Hill development is also important
to the overall success of the development. These uses should be located at or
near major intersections and in close proximity to I-15 interchanges.
      3.   Open space, parks, urban plazas, and trails that connect these
elements are also vital to the success of Falcon Hill. Ensuring the long term
vision and placement of these facilities and the overall pedestrian walkability
is of the upmost importance.
      4.   See
Standard Drawing 6P-01.
6.03   Street Hierarchy
   A.   Provide access to Falcon Hill and Hill Air Force Base with minimal
impact to vehicular traffic on the freeway interchanges and adjacent access
roadways.
      1.   Reconstruct gate entrances to be more functional with the ability to
process entry to the base more rapidly without compromising Base security
standards.
      2.   To accommodate greater traffic flow, entrance roads should be
sufficiently designed.
      3.   Adequate stacking should be provided for trucks and other vehicles
waiting to pass through the HAFB security gates.
   B.   Establish a road and traffic circulation system that provides access to
all portions of the site and accommodates traffic generated from the various on
site uses
      1.   Roadways should be of sufficient widths and lane capacities to move
traffic through the site without unreasonable delays.
      2.   Accommodations for mass transit and alternative modes of
transportation should be a primary consideration when designing roadways.
      3.   Stacking lanes and distances should be sufficient to maintain proper
circulation.
   C.   Encourage and facilitate alternative transportation and circulation
within the development to reduce and mitigate vehicular traffic impacts.
      1.   Provide bicycle lanes or pathways.
      2.   Provide sidewalks along streets, between and connecting individual
buildings, parking areas, and other activity area nodes.
      3.   Encourage the implementation of parking management plans that
promote car pooling, staggered work hours, and other parking management
strategies.
6.04   Road Designations
   A.   The circulation plan at Falcon Hill consists of an engineered and
thematic hierarchy of vehicular and pedestrian routes that provide access to
individual buildings while also promoting a pedestrian oriented urban core (see
Standard Drawing 6P-02 - Street Plan).
   B.   Areas where building positioning is concentrated may take on an urban
context wherein sidewalks are widened, parking strips eliminated and replaced
with decorative paving and intermittent trees in tree wells. Turnouts for
parallel street parking, curbside loading, or bus stops are provided.
   C.   A consistent light fixture-type and spacing should be utilized along
all streets and public open spaces. (See
Standard Drawings 6D-05 and
6D-06)
   D.   A consistent tree-type should be used along each category of public
street. Decorative/ornamental trees may be used along street frontages in areas
of emphasis or of importantance.
6.05   Mass Transit and Alternative Transportation
   A.   Promote use of mass transit and alternate transportation modes.
Provisions should be made to accommodate public transportation vehicles,
bicycles, and pedestrian circulation and encourage the use thereof (refer to
Standard Drawing 6P-03 - Public Transportation Plan).
      1.   Bus Stops: Provide bus stops to facilitate the smooth flow of public
transit within the project area. Bus stops will be provided near main
intersections and other major nodes. Turn-outs from the main travel lanes will
be provided on arterial and collector roads as necessary (refer to Typical Bus
Turn-Out Detail,
Standard Drawing 6D-04).
      2.   Mass Transit: A mass transit system may extend from the north to the
West Gate area. Locations of potential transit stops are shown on the Public
Transportation Plan.
      3.   Bicycles: Additional road width should be provided to accommodate
bicycles on all road types. Bike racks and areas for bicycle parking and
storage should be provided on each site.
      4.   Pedestrian Circulation: Building sites are grouped together in pods
to allow common use of open spaces and pocket parks and to promote a campus
style pedestrian system. Each building grouping is linked with urban pedestrian
corridors enhanced with decorative hardscape, trees with tree grates, street
lighting, wayfinding signage/directories and pedestrian oriented retail
storefronts that promote pedestrian movement between buildings within building
pods, pedestrian ways between groupings of buildings, and throughout the
development.
6.06   Site Development
   A.   Building Orientation and Placement:
      1.   Buildings along roadways should be oriented to the streets and
utilize similar strategies for buffers between the street, building, and
parking.
         a.   Provide landscaped park strips and consistent landscape setbacks
behind sidewalks and between the streets and buildings.
         b.   Building entries and façades should be clearly delineated and
viewable from streets and parking or highly visible from inside designated
courtyard areas.
         c.   Provide open space and views between buildings to facilitate
wayfinding.
         d.   Minimize parking directly in front of buildings.
         e.   Relate building orientation to other structures on site or to
adjacent sites.
         f.   Consider orientation in relation to solar exposures.
         g.   Multiple buildings should be grouped around common courtyards
with interconnecting links to create business campus pods having similar
architectural characteristics.
      2.   Consider natural topography when locating buildings and parking on a
site.
         a.   Balance earth cuts and fills to the greatest extent possible.
         b.   Utilize retaining wall designs where required having a decorative
finish consistent with the buildings or other materials used on site.
   B.   Entry Location and Treatment:
      1.   Main entrances should be oriented toward the access road, street or
common plaza of the building cluster.
   C.   Connecting Walkways and Pedestrian Corridors: Provide a network of
connecting walkways between parking areas, building entries, public sidewalks,
business campuses, Urban Context, and public/private plazas and parks.
      1.   Walkways should be clearly delineated from parking areas and
oriented towards main entries to facilitate wayfinding.
      2.   Landscaping and hardscape treatments used to identify walkways
should be of similar materials throughout the development to create a sense of
harmony and consistency with a campus style theme.
      3.   Visual linkage between building entrances, parking areas and streets
should be emphasized.
      4.   Major walkways should be shaded and have decorative lighting.
   D.   Shared and Private Outdoor Space:
      1.   Provide an inviting environment by dedicating areas of open space
between clusters of buildings of adjacent sites to maintain views and openness.
Promote pedestrian nodes that front the street and public parks within business
campuses and the Urban Context.
         a.   Maintain open space between buildings on adjacent sites.
         b.   Connections between interior and exterior spaces should be
provided through the use of glass, openings, and plazas that connect interior
and exterior spaces.
         c.   Encourage the use of planters and terraced pedestrian spaces.
         d.   Provide common amenities of a consistent design for outdoor
seating, dining, or relaxation.
         e.   Combine outdoor spaces together into a single unit to make the
combined area larger.
         f.   Provide larger sized plants and tree species in areas that are
most visible to the public.
         g.   Utilize annual plantings, public art, sculpture, fountains,
raised planters, etc.
         h.   Minimize or avoid the use of solid screen walls that are not
integrated with berms, landscape screening or architecture.
   E.   Parking Strategies and Design
      1.   Create parking lots that are pleasant and convenient for users, and
as unobtrusive as possible from the street.
         a.   Generally locate the majority of parking behind or to the sides
of buildings and not between the street and buildings.
         b.   Provide shade and/or use light colored high albedo materials
(reflectance of at least 0.3).
         c.   All landscaping in parking lots should utilize materials from the
approved plant list in appropriate density to achieve designed cover within
three (3) years.
         d.   Utilize multiple tree types including ornamental, deciduous and
non-deciduous varieties.
         e.   Parking lots should be screened from streets and/or prominent
view corridors with a landscaped buffer.
         f.   Utilize plant materials that can withstand snow storage in winter
months.
      2.   Minimize the environmental effects of storm runoff and “heat
islands” from large expanses of pavement.
         a.   Provide shade trees in parking areas.
         b.   Minimize light trespass.
      3.   Provide sufficient (but not excessive) parking for buildings based
on function allowing for future flexibility.
         a.   Consider standard requirements generally published by Urban Land
Institute (ULI), ITE, or other professional standards in conjunction with any
special requirements or uses within the building.
         b.   Evaluate actual and projected building occupancies for shared
parking opportunities.
         c.   Minimize number of “reserved” parking spaces.
      4.   Accommodate the full range of expected vehicles while minimizing the
overall size of parking areas.
         a.   Provide oversized spaces for large trucks or vehicles owned and/
or maintained by building users.
         b.   Provide space for bicycle and motorcycle parking.
      5.   Provide proper access to parking, maintain good traffic flow, and
minimize the number of curb cuts onto roadways.
         a.   Wherever possible curb cuts and driveways should be shared or
common between multiple buildings.
         b.   Driveways should generally be perpendicular to the streets.
         c.   The distance between curb cuts and from corners to curb cuts
shall be sufficient to maintain safe minimum clear visual views as determined
by speed limit of adjacent street.
         d.   Landscaping within the site distance view-shed of parking entries
shall be lower than three (3) feet and/or higher than seven (7) feet above
finished grade.
         e.   Design accesses to major parking areas similar to street
intersections to maximize traffic flow.
      6.   Utilize structured parking to increase building density in urban
core areas and for high-rise buildings. Minimize any negative visual and
physical impacts.
         a.   Do not use parking structures as a part of the frontage of a
building unless the façade is architecturally integrated.
         b.   Locate parking structures behind buildings where possible.
         c.   Structured parking may be below and/or above grade.
         d.   Parking structures should be compatible in massing, scale and
materials with the buildings they support or surround.
         e.   Incorporate landscape material, such as vines and/or planters to
soften the edges of parking structures unless prohibited by Anti-Terrorist
Force Protection (ATFP).
         f.   Take advantage of slopes on site, where possible, to minimize the
appearance of a parking structure by setting it into an embankment of a sloped
site.
      7.   Combine parking areas for multiple buildings and minimize parking
requirements by evaluating peak parking demands of various uses.
         a.   Use Urban Land Institute’s Shared Parking Standards, or
equivalent, to calculate the total number of shared parking spaces.
         b.   Eliminate barriers between parking lots except where security
requirements dictate otherwise.
      8.   Create incentives that promote sustainable transportation options
especially for commuting employees.
         a.   Provide bicycle/motorcycle parking for a minimum of three percent
(3%) of all regular building occupants.
         b.   Provide customer/visitor parking in highly visible locations.
         c.   Locate and designate parking for energy efficient cars, carpool
or vanpool vehicles close to buildings entrances.
         d.   Provide VIP parking for special guests, clients and military
officers.
         e.   Implement parking management plans.
   F.   Service and Loading Areas:
      1.   Reduce the visual and functional impact of service areas on public
spaces.
         a.   Locate service and loading areas away from public access ways.
         b.   Provide buffer for service, recycling and delivery areas from the
primary public access points by using berms, landscape material (mature within
two (2) years), walls, etc. Avoid using blank walls and/or solid fences.
         c.   Clearly mark pedestrian routes within the service area.
         d.   Provide adequate loading and maneuvering space for trucks and
other service vehicles.
         e.   Use shared service areas with adjacent buildings if possible.
         f.   Provide enclosures compatible in style and materials with the
primary building(s) to house dumpsters, recycling bins, etc.
         g.   To screen service areas use materials and details that are
complementary to the primary and secondary materials chosen for the building.
         h.   Provide turn-out zones or signed loading areas for delivery
trucks, or other temporary drop-off/pick-up.
6.07   Utilities
   A.   Reduce the visual impact of utility equipment while accommodating its
placement and function.
      1.   Avoid to the extent possible locating utility boxes and other above-
grade equipment in prominent locations within the project (i.e., at a public
entrance to a building, in a plaza space, etc.).
      2.   Cluster and screen utility boxes, transformers, etc., where
possible.
      3.   Locate equipment away from public thoroughfares and discreetly place
away from public entrances to buildings.
   B.   Design and install utility network infrastructure sufficiently sized to
accommodate anticipated needs of the project.
      1.   Comply with MIDA and/or HAFB requirements as applicable for each
utility system.
      2.   Coordinate with power, gas, internet, telephone, and other utility
companies to ensure adequacy and availability of these services.
      3.   Provide infrastructure components and easements as required.
6.08   Sustainability
   A.   Create environmentally sustainable projects, utilize energy efficient
building designs, preserve natural resources, provide indoor environmental
quality, and innovation in the design/construction process.
      1.   Meet the minimum level of Leadership in Enegry and Environmental
Design (LEED) that may be certifiable.
      2.   Use the LEED Score Sheet on
Standard Drawing 6P-04 to show what sustainable criteria will be incorporated
in each project.
      3.   Achieve a minimum of twenty-six (26) points in sustainable criteria
spanning the range of sustainable site, water efficiency, energy and
atmosphere, materials and resources, indoor environmental quality, and
innovation and design process.
   B.   Use environmentally friendly renewable and regional material to the
extent practical that may be used toward LEED points accumulation.
      1.   Incorporate salvaged, refurbished, reused, post-consumer recycled
and post-industrial content materials products or furnishings.
      2.   Recycled content materials shall be defined in accordance with the
Federal Trade Commission document. Guides for the Use of Environmental
Marketing Claims, 16 CFR 260.7(e) is available at www.ftc.gov/bcp/grnrule/
guides980427.htm.
      3.   Incorporate rapidly renewable building materials and products (made
from plants that are typically harvested within a ten- (10)-year cycle or
shorter).
      4.   Incorporate building materials and products that are manufactured or
assembled regionally within a radius of three hundred fifty (350) miles.
   C.   Use recycling and other responsible waste management practices for
construction and long-term project management.
      1.   Provide easily accessible areas serving each project area dedicated
to the separation, collection and storage of materials for recycling including
paper, corrugated cardboard, glass, plastics and metals.
      2.   Develop and implement a waste management plan, quantifying material
diversion goals.
      3.   Provide separate recycling bins in trash enclosures areas out of
view from major pedestrian corridors.
6.09   Architectural Design
   A.   Building Design Intent: The size and footprint of the individual
buildings may vary depending on use of the building and shape of the lot.
However, the materials, textures, and colors of each building façade should be
of the high quality and reflect the professionalism of Falcon Hill. Only high
quality materials should be permitted.. Color, texture and architectural
elements should be used to emphasize entrances and break the monotony of large
vertical surfaces. Screening or retaining walls should complement the colors
and materials used on the building..
   B.   Building Orientation: Emphasize sight lines and open spaces that
influence pedestrian wayfinding from one building to another and from parking
to points of entry.
      1.   Maximize solar advantage where possible.
      2.   Relate building orientation to other structures on site or to
adjacent sites.
      3.   Building orientation and the location of entrances should be such to
encourage visual wayfinding and minimize the need for exterior directional
signage.
      4.   Main entrances shall be a dominant feature of the building and
highly recognizable from the public roadways.
      5.   Secondary public entrances shall be an identifiable feature on the
building from the parking lot while being subsidiary to the main entrance.
      6.   Functional entrances such as stair exiting shall be subsidiary to
the primary and secondary entrances while maintaining consistency with the
architecture of the building.
      7.   Entry hardscape materials shall utilize similar thematic materials
as those used along the streetscapes to create a sense of visual and thematic
harmony.
   C.   Glazing and Fenestration: Provide advantage for interior spaces with
views, day lighting and solar design to enhance the visual expression of the
exterior of the building.
      1.   Incorporate creative design solutions that reflect the
professionalism of the park as a whole and do not detract or create design
inconsistencies between buildings.
      2.   Consider views that collaborate with exterior fenestration and lead
to natural day lighting conditions for the occupants
      3.   Consider interior day lighting and solar advantages as they relate
to creating exterior design solutions.
      4.   Design glazing areas that naturally draw the eye toward points of
entry.
      5.   Avoid long lengths of walls (horizontally and vertically) without
window treatment.
      6.   Avoid long lengths of walls without steps in plane unless supported
by a strong design concept.
      7.   Choose glass color (if not clear) that is in harmony with other
building materials and natural character of the region.
      8.   Glazing and fenestration details should be consistent around the
entire building.
   D.   Roof Form and Building Massing: Design roofs with careful attention to
articulation of mass and scale. Roof mass and form should be proportionate to
the building and its surroundings as well as contribute to the relationship of
the site.
      1.   Generally use flat roofs except, possibly, at decorative or feature
areas.
      2.   Differing roof heights or perceptions of differing roof heights are
encouraged to scale down the uni-dimensional mass of flat roofs.
      3.   Use massing that fluctuates and breaks down large areas of uniform
heights.
      4.   Avoid roof shapes that allude to a style not in harmony with the
professionalism of the park.
      5.   Select roof materials and colors that are in harmony with building
design.
      6.   Create details at eaves, connection to windows, and intersection
with adjoining roofs that are authentic.
      7.   Harmonize overhangs and canopies which protect occupants from the
elements with established design direction.
      8.   Roof design should contribute to a safe and hospitable environment.
   E.   Structural Elements: Appropriately express structural elements in
building designs where such elements reinforce the overall building design
scheme.
      1.   Express structural bracing for lateral (wind and seismic) loads when
appropriate.
      2.   Utilize consistent details throughout the building in the expression
of structure and make them harmonious with the overall architectural character.
      3.   Keep the expression of structural elements simple and honest; avoid
the use of faux structural members.
      4.   The overall appearance, scale, and effects of structural expression
should be proportionate to the mass and load perceived to be carried.
   F.   Building Façades: Create building design that has a relationship to the
regional terrain, a sense of authenticity, and use of materials that are
durable and have natural characteristics.
      1.   Architectural emphasis should be given to all sides of the
structure.
      2.   Take a design approach which encourages new technology, timeless
architecture and a sense of permanence and stability.
      3.   Use a material pallet that is consistent with surrounding buildings.
      4.   Create line, color, and texture that unify the buildings as a part
of the whole project.
      5.   Keep material use uncluttered and straightforward. Avoid the use of
too many materials on a single building or façade.
      6.   Use simple and clean details, trims, embellishments, etc., that are
consistent with the type of material being used.
      7.   Heavier materials, i.e., precast concrete, stone, should begin at
the lower levels and transition to lighter materials such as glass and metal,
if any, at the upper levels.
   G.   Corner Treatments:
      1.   Maintain a continuation and consistency of façade treatment and
materials around the corners of the building.
      2.   Transitions of materials from primary façades to secondary façades
can be accomplished through architectural features.
   H.   Façade Materials: Have a consistent standard for the use of varying
materials and detailing on multiple buildings within the Falcon Hill project
area.
      1.   Use details and features that work well with the chosen primary and
secondary building materials.
      2.   Design with integrity such that details are visually true and
consistent with their materials of construction.
      3.   Use materials that are consistent to the design intentions that have
been previously established as well as using materials in a manner that is true
to their practical use.
      4.   Natural Materials are encouraged, however painted materials may be
used upon the review of MIDA staff, the DRC and with the approval of the MIDA
Board.
   I.   Façade Material Definitions:
      1.   Primary Building Façade Materials: Should comprise at least sixty
percent (60%) of the primary building façade and forty percent (40%) on other
façades, not including window or door openings.
         a.   Architectural Concrete: Includes precast panels or formed in
place, with special textures and finishes.
         b.   Architectural Metal Panels: Includes ribbed or smooth, natural or
pre-finished metal panel wall systems.
      2.   Secondary Building Façade Materials: May comprise up to forty
percent (40%) of the primary façade, not including window and door openings,
and up to sixty percent (60%) of secondary building façade.
         a.   Architectural Glass: Includes curtain walls, ribbon windows and
spandrel glazing.
         b.   Stucco: Includes cement stucco and exterior insulated finish
systems (EIFS) of varying textures.
   J.   Façade Material Requirements
      1.   Architectural Precast Concrete:
         a.   Material should be used in a manner that achieves a sense of
permanence and quality.
         b.   Architectural concrete panels can be approved based on finish,
color, and amount of surface covered.
         c.   Tilt-up panels may be used on flex type buildings and in
conjunction with parking decks.
      2.   Architectural Metal Panels:
         a.   Architectural metal panels are approved in conjunction with
precast concrete.
         b.   Ribbed panels may not be utilized on vertical surfaces unless
totally integrated with a roof element.
         c.   Architectural metal panels may be utilized as a primary façade
material.
      3.   Architectural Glass and Framing:
         a.   Architectural glass is approved based upon system, performance
and color.
         b.   Where framing materials are exposed, utilize a consistent neutral
color compatible with the glass or standard anodized finish.
         c.   Highly reflective glass should not be used.
      4.   Concrete Block Masonry:
         a.   Standard concrete block masonry will not generally be allowed.
         b.   CMU having decorative, textured, or scored finishes may be used
provided appropriate detailing and fenestration is incorporated.
         c.   Special, ceramic glazed or custom CMU units and jumbo sizes
available to mimic natural stone panels may be used.
      5.   Stucco:
         a.   Use in a simple manner over larger planar wall surfaces.
         b.   Shall be kept to a minimum and be avoided on primary building
façades.
         c.   Stucco turns corners without need of trim, so keep clean lines at
the corners.
         d.   Details such as quoins, lintels, keystones are not allowed.
         e.   Varying finishes are allowed, but should generally have smooth,
metallic, or natural stone-like appearance.
         f.   Provide minimum one- (1)-inch joints in EIFS panels at
appropriate locations to make a façade element appear as a panelized wall with
individual sections.
         g.   Natural stuccos shall have expansion joints to express individual
panels and prevent cracking.
      6.   Wood:
         a.   Exposed wood construction is not allowed on office buildings.
         b.   Wood may be used as an accent feature on retail buildings.
         c.   Any other type of exposed wood construction may be considered on
a case by case basis.
      7.   Paint or Stain:
         a.   Painted or stained surfaces are not generally allowed. However,
approval for painted or stained surfaces may be granted following a review by
MIDA staff and the DRC and when approved by the MIDA board.
   K.   Downspouts
      1.   Exposed downspouts shall not be allowed on the primary façade or on
façades facing streets.
      2.   Shall appropriately convey primary stormwater run off from any
building or structure to an appropriate outfall point such as landscaped
detention/percolation basins or underground conveyance.
6.10   Landscape Design
   A.   The purpose of the Landscape Design portion of this Chapter is to
provide standards, guidelines, and strategies for site development, as site
landscape is a critical visual element that will define the overall visual
character of the business park. The intent is to preserve the special
attributes, and, to the fullest extent possible, minimize adverse effects on
the natural environment. Professional landscape treatments will create a
continuous landscape character that extends throughout the business park.
   B.   Planting Zones: Two (2) planting zones have been defined for the Site
Plan area where the intensity of landscape treatment reflects the significance
of the applicable areas. These zones are:
      1.   Intensive Planting Zone: Areas with the greatest variety of plant
materials and the highest irrigation requirements. This zone is used in areas
where a site element is featured or significant design detail is warranted.
This includes primary street intersections, drives, building entrances, and
high pedestrian use areas.
      2.   Transitional Planting Zone: Areas that provide subtle connections
between the Intensive Zone and the surrounding area. The design character
within this zone needs to provide a gradual transition from the intensive
interior to the exterior of each developed area. Plant materials within this
zone are a unique combination that reflects each adjacent zone. The use of
berming in this zone is particularly effective.
   C.   Individual Building Sites: There is to be a concentric intensity of
landscape centered around each office/building development. That is, the
greatest intensity of an introduced landscape is to occur in the immediate
vicinity of the building and transition back to the Transitional Zone as the
landscape progresses towards the perimeter of the site. Around the perimeter of
each individual building site it is the intent that there will be a visual
continuity between the surrounding buildings, open space areas, and pedestrian
corridors. These introduced landscaped areas are to be in conjunction with
building entrances, pedestrian spaces, employee break areas, and traditional
outdoor eating areas. The landscape treatment of these areas should use water
conservation measures while providing the necessary amenities of the business
park.
   D.   Phasing Boundaries:
      1.   Adjacent to open space or any non-irrigated area there shall be a
Transitional Zone between parcels so that a uniform appearance is created. The
Transition Zone shall have undulations and be coordinated across Site Plan
boundaries. The open space shall extend into the adjoining parcel for thirty
percent (30%) of the length of the property line. The plant palette and the
organic mulch shall be similar to the materials used in the adjoining parcels.
   E.   Public Plazas and Gathering Places:
      1.   Memorial Park, located near the West Gate entrance, is intended as a
tribute to the role of HAFB in protecting and serving our country. It features
the “missing man” formation by means of a sculptural representation and serves
as a place of honor, patriotism and respect for the men and women who have
served our country at HAFB (see
Standard Drawing 6P-05 Thematic Project Imagery).
      2.   Museum Park is located near the Roy entrance and is integrated with
and across from the existing museum public space. Appropriate thematic imagery,
sculptural elements, and public amenities will be provided.
      3.   Gate Park is located at the 1800 North/Falcon Hill intersection.
Appropriate thematic imagery, sculptural elements and public amenities will be
provided.
   F.   Visual Buffers: Where utility, service, garbage and/or loading areas
face adjacent public streets and/or open spaces these facilities are to be
thoroughly screened. Earth berming integrated with plant material is encouraged
where possible. The plant material should be a combination of evergreen trees
and large deciduous shrubs planted in an informal configuration. The use of
opaque structures used alone or in combination with landscaping is also
recommended.
   G.   Landscape Setbacks: Uses permitted within landscape setbacks are berms,
driveways crossings, landscaping, public and private utilities, drainage and
slopes, sidewalks, trails, irrigation, and permitted signs. Refer to Appendix
A, Chapter 7 - Roadway Design Standards for landscape setback dimensions.
   H.   Median Design: Landscape themes for medians will be specific to roadway
corridors, creating a distinct yet cohesive theme for the different corridors.
Quality median design is encouraged through the combination of materials like
stamped concrete, pavers, stone, berms and plantings. Within the medians, the
plant material is to be grouped together to form informal groupings and other
natural looking configurations. Where medians are provided, they should be
comprised of plants from the Transitional Plant Palette. The use of berming and
large massing of plant material is encouraged. Landscaping shall properly be
contained by the curbing.
   I.   Intersections: Landscape at intersections is based on a hierarchy of
road type. The greatest emphasis will be at major arterial intersections or
interchanges, with the least emphasis at the Industrial/Industrial street
intersections. Major arterial intersections will receive the greatest traffic
volumes and, particularly if grade separated, will be experienced at higher
rates of speed. Larger plant material masses are necessary in order to provide
visual interest. Secondary intersections are smaller in scale and are the
character of the Transitional Zone.
   J.   Urban Context:
      1.   Nodes having a more urban character are intended near main
intersections along Falcon Hill Drive and major access roads to the project.
Floor area ratios should be greater, buildings placed closer together and
closer to the street, and sidewalks made wider. Decorative concrete paving,
street furnishings, raised planters, feature landscaping, and other amenities
should be incorporated. On-street parking and loading zones may be provided in
expanded road width pockets. Refer to
Standard Drawing 6D-03.
   K.   Landscape and Hardscape Standards:
      1.   Arterial and Collector Road.
         a.   Major Tree Type: Ulmus parvifolia ‘Allee’ - Allee Elm.
         b.   Accent Tree Type: Prunus cerasifera ‘Mount St. Helen’ - Mount
Saint Helen Flowering Plum.
         c.   Landscape Median Tree Type: Zelkova serrata ‘Musashino’.
         d.   Tree Spacing: Thirty (30) feet oc. or if grouped together to
provide views, the total number of trees required shall be equal to the length
of road multiplied by thirty (30) feet on both sides of the road.
         e.   Sidewalk Details: Refer to Arterial Road Cross-Section –
Standard Drawing 6D-01.
      2.   Local Road – Others:
         a.   Major Tree Type: May be any of the following but only one of the
species per street: Acer tatarica – Tartaian Maple, Koelreuteria paniclata-
Golden Rain Tree, Pyrus calleriana ‘Bradford’ Bradford Flowering Pear.
         b.   Accent Tree Type: Prunus cerasifera ‘Mount St. Helen’ - Mount
Saint Helen Flowering Plum.
         c.   Tree Spacing: Thirty (30) feet oc. or if grouped together to
provide views, the total number of trees required shall be equal to the length
of road multiplied by thirty (30) feet on both sides of the road.
         d.   Sidewalk Details: Refer to Local Road Cross-Section –
Standard Drawing 6D-02.
      3.   Urban Context:
         a.   Major Tree Type: Same as that used for the respective street
type, but placed in tree well with tree grate.
         b.   Tree Spacing: Trees in Tree Grates generally thirty (30) feet oc.
but coordinated with street lighting and site furnishings. Spacing for all
other urban context tree type, varies depending on context.
         c.   Sidewalk Details: Refer to Urban Context Hardscape –
Standard Drawing 6D-03.
         d.   Tree Grate: Neenah Foundry, Distinctive Creations Model Number R-
8724-A.
         e.   Decorative Concrete: Bomanite, Sandscape Texture, 750 Desert Tan
1% Loading.
   L.   Irrigation:
      1.   Irrigation systems should be zoned so those plant materials with
similar water demands are on the same irrigation zone, for example, high water
use turf should be separated from shrubs and ornamental grasses. Professionally
designed and drawn irrigation plans should be an integral part of each new
building or landscape plan. The plans should specifically address application
methods, natural precipitation, and application rates for the individual zones.
Plans also need to indicate tap locations, controller type, type and size of
heads, drip methods, type and size of mainline, laterals, water pressure, road
crossing sleeves, and meter locations.
      2.   Irrigation Consumption (Limitations): Where available, non-potable
graywater irrigation water is to be used. Permanent underground irrigation
systems are permitted in improved landscape areas and in areas of re-vegetation
for establishment purposes. Best management practices should be used and
standard maintenance principles applied to ensure proper plant health through
all seasons of the year. Materials and products shall comply with the adopted
standards.
6.11   Lighting and Night Sky Protection
   A.   Site and Building Exfiltration: Reduce light pollution, glare and
energy waste by directing light downward and to appropriate areas of need, and
using appropriate light levels.
      1.   Meet or provide lower light levels and uniformity ratios than those
recommended by the Illuminating Engineering Society of North America (IESNA).
Recommended Practice Manual: Lighting for Exterior Environments (RP-33-99).
      2.   Design exterior lighting such that all exterior luminaires with more
than one thousand (1,000) initial lamp lumens are shielded and all luminaires
with more than three thousand five hundred (3,500) initial lamp lumens meet the
Full Cutoff IESNA Classification.
      3.   The maximum candela value of all interior lighting shall fall within
the building (not through windows).
      4.   The maximum candela value of all exterior lighting shall fall within
the property. Any luminaire within a distance of two and one-half (2.5) times
its mounting height from the property boundary shall have shielding so no light
from that luminaire crosses the property boundary.
   B.   Night Sky Protection: Employ proper methods of energy-efficient
lighting that will establish both safety and community identity while
respecting the night sky.
      1.   Select fixtures that are shielded or filtered to minimize ambient
light and to preserve night views.
      2.   Provide lighting at driveways, intersections, and hazard areas.
      3.   Lighting shall be directed downward at all times (up-lighting is
prohibited).
      4.   Except for decorative lighting on buildings or building entrances,
shield and filter light rays to prevent light from emitting above the
horizontal plane.
      5.   The following shall be submitted for review and approval:
         a.   Locations, type of illuminating devices, fixtures, lamps,
supports, reflectors, and other devices.
         b.   Description of the illuminating devices, fixtures, lamps,
supports, reflectors and other devices. The description may include (but is not
limited to) manufacturers’ catalog cut sheets and drawings.
         c.   Photometric data, such as that provided by manufacturers, showing
the angle of cutoff for light emissions.
      6.   A change request must be submitted to substitute any outdoor light
fixture or the type of light source after approval has been issued.
      7.   Solar power, photovoltaic cells and/or motion detectors should be
employed to conserve energy.
      8.   Increase light levels where required by base security and public
safety.
      9.   Non-reflective surfaces below lighted areas should be used in order
to minimize light reflecting into the sky.
      10.   Use energy-efficient lamps, which provide appropriate lighting
levels.
      11.   Site lighting should be directed onto vegetation or prominent site
features.
   C.   Street Lighting:
      1.   Lighting shall occur at all intersections, along all arterials, and
hazard areas. A lighting plan shall be submitted to MIDA for review and
approval.
      2.   General street lighting utilizes fixture-type TX-1 on thirty- (30)-
feet high steel light poles having a natural aluminum painted finish.
      3.   Pedestrian walkways and plazas utilize fixture-type TX-2 on fifteen-
(15)-foot high light poles having a natural aluminum painted finish.
      4.   Space fixtures on public streets at approximately sixty- (60)-feet
on center. Adjust spacing as required in coordination with street trees,
driveways, and intersections.
      5.   For on-base street lighting, utilize fixture-type TX-3 on 30-ft high
steel light poles having a dark bronze painted finish.
      6.   Materials Standards:
         a.   Fixture Type TX-1: Lithonia Aeris, Decorative Curved Arm, AS2
with two hundred (200) - four hundred (400)-watt metal halide lamps generally
in single arm configuration except where fixtures are located in center
medians; natural aluminum finish. Refer to
Standard Drawing 6D-05.
         b.   Fixture Type TX-2: Lithonia Aeris, Decorative Curved Arm, AS1
with thirty-five (35) - one hundred seventy-five (175)-watt metal halide lamps
generally in single arm configuration except where fixtures are located where
equivalent illumination is desired on both sides; natural aluminum finish.
Refer to
Standard Drawing 6D-06.
         c.   Fixture Type TX-3: Use one of the HAFB designated standard
fixtures. Similar to
Standard Drawing 6D-06.
         d.   Lighted Sidewalk Bollards: Lithonia Aeris ASB with smooth cross
or crowned top; natural aluminum finish.
6.12   Signage
   A.   Signs add visual character and aesthetic appeal to mixed use centers
while informing drivers and pedestrians of what exists within a given area.
      1.   Freeway Oriented Project Sign:
         a.   Freeway oriented project signs should consist of architectural,
hardscape, landscape and lighting materials consistent with materials used in
public plaza areas that symbolize the thematic elements of Falcon Hill as it
relates to HAFB and the Aerospace Industry.
      2.   Wayfinding Signs: Allow signs that aid in wayfinding and direct
traffic without becoming distracting (example graphics/photos).
         a.   Wayfinding signs sould be limited to communicating the general
location and direction of amenities or facilities, such as parking, exits,
delivery, or loading areas and may be monument oriented, projecting from retail
building overhangs and awnings, retail window signs, pedestal-mounted and/or
post mounted signs.
         b.   Lettering height for wayfinding signs is subject to approval on a
case by case basis.
         c.   Wayfinding signs are subject to review and approval of MIDA if
outside the HAFB boundary.
         d.   Locate regulatory signs such as speed limit, street name, ADA
access, no parking, loading and other related signs consistent with governing
jurisdiction and traffic safety standards in coordination with other site
improvements.
      3.   Lettering and Illumination: Ensure attractive and durable signage
that is harmonious in character with the surroundings.
         a.   Scale and proportion of graphics shall be compatible with the
building’s architectural character.
         b.   Exterior lettering that is exposed to the weather shall be
mounted at least ¾” from the building wall to permit proper drainage.
         c.   External light sources shall be screened from view.
         d.   Illumination systems shall minimize energy usage by utilizing
contemporary energy saving techniques and materials.
      4.   Seasonal or Coordinated Event Pennants and Banners (Temporary
Signs): Allow signs that promote special events.
         a.   Banners shall not exceed a width of three (3) feet or a height of
six (6) feet.
         b.   Temporary signs shall be removed at the end of events or thirty
(30) days, whichever is sooner.
         c.   Banners, pennants, flags and any other advertising devices
(except floodlights and balloons) may be placed on an occupant’s property
subject to MIDA’s signage guidelines.
         d.   The total area of all temporary signs and advertising devices do
not exceed the area allowed for permanent signage.
         e.   Temporary signs shall not be located in a manner or place where
permanent signs are prohibited.
         f.   Temporary signs placed within the public road easement may be
civic or commercial in nature, but must be part of a coordinated sponsored
event as approved by the MIDA Board.
   B.   A uniform sign criteria shall be established for each development area.
The criteria shall establish the location and specific design of monument
signage, the character and materials to be used for wall signs, and include
other descriptive information, illustrations, photographic examples, and
graphics.

   Standard Drawing 6D-06
CHAPTER 7 ROADWAY DESIGN STANDARDS
7.01   Purpose
7.02   Definitions
7.03   General Standards
7.04   Roadway Design
7.05   Special Notes
7.01   Purpose
The purpose of this Chapter is to provide engineering standards for the design
of all roadways within the Falcon Hill project. These roadways are to be
constructed in a manner that they can be efficiently maintained, provide
longevity of the road structure, and provide for the safety of the public.
7.02   Definitions
As used in this Chapter:
      Arterial Road: A moderate or high-capacity road entering the Falcon Hill
site from Interstate 15 with limited access points.
      Collector Road: A low or moderate-capacity road connecting the Arterial
Roads from the north end to the south end of the site with multiple access
points.
      High Volume Driveway: An entrance to large parking areas that will
experience high volumes of traffic. These entrances will generally be located
where right turn pockets are used to divert traffic out of the main flow for
Arterial and Collector Roads (See Standard Drawing 7D-07)
      Local Road: A low-capacity road that will typically connect to a
Collector Road with the primary purpose of accessing abutting properties.
      Low Volume Driveway: A standard driveway approach typically used to
access restaurants, retail buildings, small office buildings, and commercial
hospitality buildings (See Standard Drawing 7D-07).
7.03   General Standards
The standards outlined in this section can be supplemented by AASHTO (American
Association of State Highway and Transportation Officials), A Policy on
Geometric Design of Highways and Streets. In cases of conflict, a determination
shall be made by the MIDA Engineer.
7.04   Roadway Design
   A.   Radii of street corners to fit the back of sidewalk and back of curb
requirements per
Standard Drawing 7D-01.
   B.   Access ramps are to be located at all road crossings and conform to
Standard Drawings
7D-02 and
7D-03. All access ramps shall be constructed to comply to a minimum standard as
established by: The Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA), Americans with
Disabilities Accessibility Guidelines (ADAAG), U.S. Federal Highway
Administration, and the Manual on Uniform Traffic Control Devices (MUTCD).
   C.   All roadway curb and gutter are to be thirty (30) inches concrete
standard curb and gutter per
Standard Drawing 7D-04.
   D.   All curb walls are to be six (6) inches standard curb wall per
Standard Drawing 7D-04.
   E.   Raised medians shall be constructed per
Standard Drawing 7D-05.
   F.   Concrete sidewalk to be a minimum of five (5) feet wide and shall be
constructed per
Standard Drawing 7D-06.
   G.   As a minimum, roadway cross-sections are to conform to
Standard Drawing 7D-07.
   H.   Construct drive approaches per
Standard Drawing 7D-08.
   I.   Roadway Widening: All roadway widening to include curb and gutter,
parkstrip, and sidewalk per this chapter. In addition, the following must be
adhered to:
      1.   Saw-cut edge of existing road pavement one (1) foot back of existing
edge of asphalt.
      2.   In all cases, space must be provided between the existing edge of
asphalt and the curb and gutter in order to provide proper compaction of
roadway structure with a plate compactor.
      3.   Cross slopes of additional pavement are to be two percent (2%)
minimum and four percent (4%) maximum.
   J.   Vertical Design of Roadways:
      1.   The vertical alignment shall be such as to minimize grade breaks
along the centerline and curb lines.
      2.   The algebraic difference of a grade break shall not exceed two
percent (2%). Eliminate grade breaks in excess of the above criteria by means
of a vertical curve of fifty (50) feet. All vertical curve lengths shall be
dependant upon three (3) factors:
         a.   Design speed.
         b.   Algebraic differences in grades.
         c.   Design constant (k).
      3.   Minimum gutter slope allowed is four-tenths percent (0.4%).
      4.   Maximum longitudinal slope along centerline shall be eight percent
(8%).
      5.   Intersecting street angles may vary between eighty-five (85) and
ninety-five (95) degrees.
      6.   As a minimum, the Geotechnical and Soils Report required in
Appendix A, Chapter 2 must contain the following:
         a.   Soil borings along roadway centerline and other areas as needed.
         b.   Analysis on the overall bearing capacity of the soil.
         c.   Recommendation for structural street cross section.
         d.   Cut and fill slope requirements.
         e.   Compaction requirements.
   K.   Horizontal Design of Roadways:
      1.   Curve data is required for all centerline and curb line curves and
also for all curb returns at intersections.
      2.   Minimum centerline radius for Collectors and Arterials shall be
based on the design speed, but in no case shall be less than a three hundred
(300) foot radius unless otherwise approved by the MIDA Engineer.
      3.   Minimum tangent between curves with a length of twice the right-of-
way width is required along the centerline of all public roads.
      4.   Temporary turnarounds shall be required on all streets which shall
be extended in the future and which exceed one hundred fifty (150) lineal feet
from the centerline intersections of the closest intersecting street.
Additional rights-of-way or easements necessary to construct and maintain the
temporary turnaround are also required.
      5.   The horizontal alignment should be straight through the
intersections, but where horizontal curves cannot be avoided, the following
should be observed:
         a.   Use a curve of sufficient radius to provide adequate sight
distance and minimize the need for superelevation.
         b.   Do not begin or end a curve within an intersection.
         c.   Eliminate angle points in excess of two (2) degrees on major or
secondary roads by use of a large radius curve.
         d.   Angle points up to five (5) degrees are permissible at the
intersection of two Local roads.
         e.   Curve radii and superelevation should consider the design speed
for the given road.
      6.   Cul-de-sacs shall not be longer than six hundred (600) feet from the
centerline of the adjoining street to the center of the cul-de-sac. This length
may be exceeded where mitigating measures are taken for emergency response. The
design of a cul-de-sac street shall pay particular attention to traffic
volumes, especially where lengths exceed six hundred (600) feet.
      7.   Where a street longer than one hundred fifty (150( feet is designed
to remain only temporarily as a dead-end street, an adequate turning area shall
be provided as follows:
      8.   Dead end streets of length greater than one hundred fifty (150) feet
shall be required to have an asphalt pavement surface turn-around with a
minimum outside radius of sixty (60) feet in the closed end.
      9.   Temporary ends of street in phased development must provide the
width and asphalt pavement but may omit curb and gutter on a turnaround.
      10.   All roads shall be designed for a right turn lane of a minimum one
hundred (100) feet except where the road ends in a parking area.
   L.   MIDA reserves the right to name any and all streets. Building numbers
shall be assigned in accordance with the numbering system devised by MIDA.
   M.   Driveways:
      1.   All driveway approaches shall meet the specifications below as well
as those shown in
Standard Drawing 7D-07:
         a.   Low Volume Driveway:
            1.   Minimum Width - twenty-four (24) feet.
            2.   Minimum Width for one way access - twenty (20) feet.
            3.   Maximum Width - thirty-six (36) feet.
            4.   Minimum Concrete Thickness - eight (8) inches.
            5.   Minimum Base Course - six (6) inches.
         b.   High Volume Driveway:
            1.   Minimum Asphalt Width - twenty-four (24) feet.
            2.   Maximum Asphalt Width - forty (40) feet.
            3.   Minimum Asphalt Thickness - four (4) inches.
            4.   Minimum Base Course - six (6) inches.
            5.   Where required, the minimum granular borrow thickness shall be
eight (8) inches.
      2.   All driveways shall be separated from each other by at least fifty
(50) feet as measured from nearest edges of travel ways. For a High Volume
Driveway, the edge of the travel way shall be the edge of asphalt. For Low
Volume Driveways, the measurement will be at the location where the flare
intersects the sidewalk.
      3.   All driveways shall be offset from other driveways by no less than
twice the flare width as per AASHTO Specifications.
         a.   Engineering judgment shall override the recommended dimensions
set forth if warranted by specific traffic conditions. Special consideration
from MIDA’s Engineer is required.
         b.   For corner properties, access to streets shall be provided from
the lesser (lowest functional classification) street.
         c.   Corner clearance requirements for access points shall meet or
exceed the minimum driveway spacing requirements.
      4.   Driveways along the property lines may be installed for shared use
of both adjacent properties only upon guarantee by a recorded access agreement.
      5.   All cut and fill slopes shall be at a maximum 3:1 unless otherwise
justified by a detailed soils investigation and approved by MIDA Engineer.
   N.   Asphalt mix design shall be submitted to MIDA’s engineer for review and
approval prior to construction. Refer to the MIDA Construction Specifications
for mix requirements.
7.05   Special Notes
The following notes shall be included on the construction drawings submitted as
part of the Site Plan application:
   A.   Contractor shall replace any existing sidewalk or curb and gutter along
the frontage of this project, that is found to be defective, as directed by the
MIDA inspector.
   B.   All public improvements, which are to be owned and maintained by MIDA
shall be constructed according to the MIDA Standards.
   C.   Notify MIDA’s Inspector, 48 hours prior to beginning construction of
any roadways or improvements, including sewer facilities, in or out of the
existing or future roadway. All inspections must be done prior to or concurrent
with construction. Failure to make this notification may result in the
uncovering and/or removal of all construction done without notification, at the
discretion of MIDA’s Engineer.
   D.   Contractor shall secure an excavation permit from MIDA.
   E.   Provide a proctor test for roadbase material to the MIDA inspector when
delivered or placed on site.
   F.   Provide estimation of quantities of excavation and fill, as well as
locations of borrow sites, surplus disposal sites, and haul routes to the MIDA
inspector.
   G.   A Professional Engineer, currently licensed in Utah, shall observe and
approve any retaining walls within the road right-of-way easement that are
higher than four (4) feet (from bottom of footing to top of wall), once
constructed. Provide a letter of approval, stamped, signed, and dated by said
Professional Engineer, to the MIDA Engineer prior to release of financial
responsibility as stated in Section
3.19.
   H.   Follow all recommendations of the approved geotechnical report. MIDA
Standards and Details shall govern, unless geotechnical report recommendations
are more stringent.
   I.   Dust, mud and erosion shall be adequately controlled and the roadway
shall be kept free of mud and debris, at all times. The use of motor oils and
other petroleum-based or toxic liquids, for dust suppression, is prohibited.
